Um, yeah… I guess the main thing is my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. After late shifts, I’ll stop and get food—like a full takeout meal—and then when I get home I just keep going. Chips, sweets, whatever’s around in the apartment.

I feel kind of stunned, honestly. Like I’ll see the takeout container and the chip bag and maybe candy wrappers, and it’s just… I don’t know, it doesn’t feel like I meant to eat all that.

Yeah… I guess I do. Not like, I don’t know, some big planned thing, but if I’ve had a really bad night with food, the next morning I’ll feel like I have to sort of fix it.

Sometimes. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.

I guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.

Like, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.

And if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.

Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.

It’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. I end up shocked by how much I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word. I even bought those meal prep containers once—this whole plan to be organized—and they’re still in the cabinet.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe some guilt, but mostly it’s this uncomfortable, “ugh, why did I do that?” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Not like, I don’t know, some big planned thing, but if I’ve had a really bad night with food, the next morning I’ll feel like I have to sort of fix it.\n\nLike I’ll go run extra miles on the trail, even if I’m wiped out from work. There’s this trail near me that I usually like, actually—it’s kind of the one place I clear my head—but after those nights it doesn’t feel relaxing. It feels more like, “you have to do this because of what you ate.”\n\nOr I’ll skip breakfast before my shift, which is dumb because then I’m tired and cranky at work, and dealing with customers when you’re hungry is… not great. But in my head it’s like I’m trying to undo the night before.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I guess. Since I moved into this apartment and things kind of settled into a routine, it’s been… usually at least once a week there’s a late night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day—or sometimes that night—I try to make up for it somehow. Like skipping breakfast, making myself do a long run even if I don’t really want to, or… sometimes throwing up. I don’t like saying that. It’s not every single day or anything, but most weeks it happens at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Sometimes. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I can point to, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I guess I do, or at least I feel that way. Like, people at the venue have told me I look too lean, and I’ll kind of nod like, “yeah, okay,” but I don’t really take it in.

If my pants feel even a little tighter before a shift, my brain just goes straight to, like, everyone’s going to notice — coworkers, guests, whoever — and they’re going to think I let myself go. Which is ridiculous, because most guests are just asking where their seats are or complaining about drink prices, but I’m standing there thinking about my waistband.

And if the scale is up even a pound, it feels like proof, even if other people are telling me the opposite. So… yeah, I don’t think I see it the way they do.

It feels more like, “you have to do this because of what you ate.”\n\nOr I’ll skip breakfast before my shift, which is dumb because then I’m tired and cranky at work, and dealing with customers when you’re hungry is… not great. But in my head it’s like I’m trying to undo the night before.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I guess. Since I moved into this apartment and things kind of settled into a routine, it’s been… usually at least once a week there’s a late night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day—or sometimes that night—I try to make up for it somehow. Like skipping breakfast, making myself do a long run even if I don’t really want to, or… sometimes throwing up. I don’t like saying that. It’s not every single day or anything, but most weeks it happens at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Sometimes. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too lean?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, or at least I feel that way. Like, people at the venue have told me I look too lean, and I’ll kind of nod like, “yeah, okay,” but I don’t really take it in.\n\nIf my pants feel even a little tighter before a shift, my brain just goes straight to, like, everyone’s going to notice — coworkers, guests, whoever — and they’re going to think I let myself go. Yeah… I think it’s more the losing control part. I mean, the weight thing is always kind of buzzing in the background, but on those nights when I start eating, it’s like I’m not fully in charge anymore.

Like I’ll tell myself, okay, just put the container away, go brush your teeth, go do literally anything else… and then I’m standing there opening another thing anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from outside my body, which sounds dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.

And then suddenly everything’s gone, and I’m just sitting there thinking, why didn’t I stop? It’s not even like I’m enjoying it by the end.

I end up shocked by how much I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word. I feel embarrassed, definitely, and kind of mad at myself, I guess. Like I’ll think, why did I spend money on all that when I’ve got groceries sitting there? I even bought those meal prep containers once—this whole plan to be organized—and they’re still in the cabinet.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe some guilt, but mostly it’s this uncomfortable, “ugh, why did I do that?” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Not like, I don’t know, some big planned thing, but if I’ve had a really bad night with food, the next morning I’ll feel like I have to sort of fix it.\n\nLike I’ll go run extra miles on the trail, even if I’m wiped out from work. There’s this trail near me that I usually like, actually—it’s kind of the one place I clear my head—but after those nights it doesn’t feel relaxing. It feels more like, “you have to do this because of what you ate.”\n\nOr I’ll skip breakfast before my shift, which is dumb because then I’m tired and cranky at work, and dealing with customers when you’re hungry is… not great. But in my head it’s like I’m trying to undo the night before.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I guess. Since I moved into this apartment and things kind of settled into a routine, it’s been… usually at least once a week there’s a late night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day—or sometimes that night—I try to make up for it somehow. Like skipping breakfast, making myself do a long run even if I don’t really want to, or… sometimes throwing up. I don’t like saying that. It’s not every single day or anything, but most weeks it happens at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Sometimes. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too lean?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, or at least I feel that way. Like, people at the venue have told me I look too lean, and I’ll kind of nod like, “yeah, okay,” but I don’t really take it in.\n\nIf my pants feel even a little tighter before a shift, my brain just goes straight to, like, everyone’s going to notice — coworkers, guests, whoever — and they’re going to think I let myself go. Which is ridiculous, because most guests are just asking where their seats are or complaining about drink prices, but I’m standing there thinking about my waistband.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it feels like proof, even if other people are telling me the opposite. So… yeah, I don’t think I see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating excessively because you're afraid of gaining weight or because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more the losing control part. I mean, the weight thing is always kind of buzzing in the background, but on those nights when I start eating, it’s like I’m not fully in charge anymore.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, okay, just put the container away, go brush your teeth, go do literally anything else… and then I’m standing there opening another thing anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from outside my body, which sounds dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nAnd then suddenly everything’s gone, and I’m just sitting there thinking, why didn’t I stop? It’s not even like I’m enjoying it by the end.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most weeks, I think… at least once. It got more noticeable after my apartment move kind of settled down, once I had a routine again. Which is funny, because I thought the routine would help, but late at night is when it happens.

I’ll mean to just have something small, and then it turns into way more than I planned. Like, past the point where I’m even hungry. And then I panic and try to make up for it — throwing up, or skipping breakfast the next morning, or making myself go on this long run even if I’m tired.

So… yeah. Usually once a week, sometimes more, but most weeks at least once.

I used to grab stuff after shifts with coworkers sometimes, but now it’s more like I’m by myself and I’ll eat way more than I meant to. Then afterward I kind of look at the wrappers or containers and I’m shocked, like… how did I eat all of that?\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after you've eaten all that food and seen the wrappers or containers?\n\nPatient: I feel kind of stunned, honestly. Like I’ll see the takeout container and the chip bag and maybe candy wrappers, and it’s just… I don’t know, it doesn’t feel like I meant to eat all that.\n\nAfter late shifts I’ll tell myself I’m just grabbing dinner, but then I get home and keep going with whatever’s around. And then I’m looking at it all afterward thinking, wow, that was a lot. It’s embarrassing, I guess. Also I hate wasting money on takeout—my apartment’s near this place that stays open late, and I keep saying I should just go straight home, but then… yeah. I end up shocked by how much I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word. I feel embarrassed, definitely, and kind of mad at myself, I guess. Like I’ll think, why did I spend money on all that when I’ve got groceries sitting there? I even bought those meal prep containers once—this whole plan to be organized—and they’re still in the cabinet.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe some guilt, but mostly it’s this uncomfortable, “ugh, why did I do that?” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Not like, I don’t know, some big planned thing, but if I’ve had a really bad night with food, the next morning I’ll feel like I have to sort of fix it.\n\nLike I’ll go run extra miles on the trail, even if I’m wiped out from work. There’s this trail near me that I usually like, actually—it’s kind of the one place I clear my head—but after those nights it doesn’t feel relaxing. It feels more like, “you have to do this because of what you ate.”\n\nOr I’ll skip breakfast before my shift, which is dumb because then I’m tired and cranky at work, and dealing with customers when you’re hungry is… not great. But in my head it’s like I’m trying to undo the night before.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I guess. Since I moved into this apartment and things kind of settled into a routine, it’s been… usually at least once a week there’s a late night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day—or sometimes that night—I try to make up for it somehow. Like skipping breakfast, making myself do a long run even if I don’t really want to, or… sometimes throwing up. I don’t like saying that. It’s not every single day or anything, but most weeks it happens at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Sometimes. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too lean?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, or at least I feel that way. Like, people at the venue have told me I look too lean, and I’ll kind of nod like, “yeah, okay,” but I don’t really take it in.\n\nIf my pants feel even a little tighter before a shift, my brain just goes straight to, like, everyone’s going to notice — coworkers, guests, whoever — and they’re going to think I let myself go. Which is ridiculous, because most guests are just asking where their seats are or complaining about drink prices, but I’m standing there thinking about my waistband.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it feels like proof, even if other people are telling me the opposite. So… yeah, I don’t think I see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating excessively because you're afraid of gaining weight or because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more the losing control part. I mean, the weight thing is always kind of buzzing in the background, but on those nights when I start eating, it’s like I’m not fully in charge anymore.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, okay, just put the container away, go brush your teeth, go do literally anything else… and then I’m standing there opening another thing anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from outside my body, which sounds dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nAnd then suddenly everything’s gone, and I’m just sitting there thinking, why didn’t I stop? It’s not even like I’m enjoying it by the end.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think… at least once. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from outside my body, which sounds dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nAnd then suddenly everything’s gone, and I’m just sitting there thinking, why didn’t I stop? It’s not even like I’m enjoying it by the end.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think… at least once. It got more noticeable after my apartment move kind of settled down, once I had a routine again. Which is funny, because I thought the routine would help, but late at night is when it happens.\n\nI’ll mean to just have something small, and then it turns into way more than I planned. Like, past the point where I’m even hungry. And then I panic and try to make up for it — throwing up, or skipping breakfast the next morning, or making myself go on this long run even if I’m tired.\n\nSo… yeah. Usually once a week, sometimes more, but most weeks at least once.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating excessively?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / entertainment_attendant_or_related_worker / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compensatory_Behavior_Purging\nmanifestation: \"Sometimes after I get home and eat too much takeout or snacks, I go to the bathroom and make myself throw up so it won’t sit in me and turn into weight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not every time, but… sometimes. Like if I get home and I’ve eaten way too much takeout or snacks, I’ll end up in the bathroom and make myself throw up.

It’s usually because I get this panicky feeling that it’s just going to sit in me and turn into weight. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know, irrational, but in the moment it feels like I have to get it out.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess the main thing is my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. After late shifts, I’ll stop and get food—like a full takeout meal—and then when I get home I just keep going. Chips, sweets, whatever’s around in the apartment.\n\nIt’s weird because I work these odd hours, and by the time I’m done everything feels like it’s either closing or it’s all fast food, you know? I used to grab stuff after shifts with coworkers sometimes, but now it’s more like I’m by myself and I’ll eat way more than I meant to. Then afterward I kind of look at the wrappers or containers and I’m shocked, like… how did I eat all of that?\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after you've eaten all that food and seen the wrappers or containers?\n\nPatient: I feel kind of stunned, honestly. Like I’ll see the takeout container and the chip bag and maybe candy wrappers, and it’s just… I don’t know, it doesn’t feel like I meant to eat all that.\n\nAfter late shifts I’ll tell myself I’m just grabbing dinner, but then I get home and keep going with whatever’s around. And then I’m looking at it all afterward thinking, wow, that was a lot. It’s embarrassing, I guess. Also I hate wasting money on takeout—my apartment’s near this place that stays open late, and I keep saying I should just go straight home, but then… yeah. I end up shocked by how much I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word. I feel embarrassed, definitely, and kind of mad at myself, I guess. Like I’ll think, why did I spend money on all that when I’ve got groceries sitting there? I even bought those meal prep containers once—this whole plan to be organized—and they’re still in the cabinet.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe some guilt, but mostly it’s this uncomfortable, “ugh, why did I do that?” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Not like, I don’t know, some big planned thing, but if I’ve had a really bad night with food, the next morning I’ll feel like I have to sort of fix it.\n\nLike I’ll go run extra miles on the trail, even if I’m wiped out from work. There’s this trail near me that I usually like, actually—it’s kind of the one place I clear my head—but after those nights it doesn’t feel relaxing. It feels more like, “you have to do this because of what you ate.”\n\nOr I’ll skip breakfast before my shift, which is dumb because then I’m tired and cranky at work, and dealing with customers when you’re hungry is… not great. But in my head it’s like I’m trying to undo the night before.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I guess. Since I moved into this apartment and things kind of settled into a routine, it’s been… usually at least once a week there’s a late night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day—or sometimes that night—I try to make up for it somehow. Like skipping breakfast, making myself do a long run even if I don’t really want to, or… sometimes throwing up. I don’t like saying that. It’s not every single day or anything, but most weeks it happens at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Sometimes. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. It’s not even like I’m enjoying it by the end.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think… at least once. It got more noticeable after my apartment move kind of settled down, once I had a routine again. Which is funny, because I thought the routine would help, but late at night is when it happens.\n\nI’ll mean to just have something small, and then it turns into way more than I planned. Like, past the point where I’m even hungry. And then I panic and try to make up for it — throwing up, or skipping breakfast the next morning, or making myself go on this long run even if I’m tired.\n\nSo… yeah. Usually once a week, sometimes more, but most weeks at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating excessively?\n\nPatient: Not every time, but… sometimes. Like if I get home and I’ve eaten way too much takeout or snacks, I’ll end up in the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s usually because I get this panicky feeling that it’s just going to sit in me and turn into weight. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… like I said, not every time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much—usually takeout or snacks, stuff that’s just there—I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself throw up.

It’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not a normal reaction, but in my head it’s like, if I don’t get it out, it’s just going to sit there and turn into weight. And then I get stuck on that thought.

Sorry, I know I’m kind of circling around it. The short answer is: sometimes.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess the main thing is my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. After late shifts, I’ll stop and get food—like a full takeout meal—and then when I get home I just keep going. Chips, sweets, whatever’s around in the apartment.\n\nIt’s weird because I work these odd hours, and by the time I’m done everything feels like it’s either closing or it’s all fast food, you know? I used to grab stuff after shifts with coworkers sometimes, but now it’s more like I’m by myself and I’ll eat way more than I meant to. Then afterward I kind of look at the wrappers or containers and I’m shocked, like… how did I eat all of that?\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after you've eaten all that food and seen the wrappers or containers?\n\nPatient: I feel kind of stunned, honestly. Like I’ll see the takeout container and the chip bag and maybe candy wrappers, and it’s just… I don’t know, it doesn’t feel like I meant to eat all that.\n\nAfter late shifts I’ll tell myself I’m just grabbing dinner, but then I get home and keep going with whatever’s around. And then I’m looking at it all afterward thinking, wow, that was a lot. It’s embarrassing, I guess. Also I hate wasting money on takeout—my apartment’s near this place that stays open late, and I keep saying I should just go straight home, but then… yeah. I end up shocked by how much I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word. I feel embarrassed, definitely, and kind of mad at myself, I guess. Like I’ll think, why did I spend money on all that when I’ve got groceries sitting there? I even bought those meal prep containers once—this whole plan to be organized—and they’re still in the cabinet.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe some guilt, but mostly it’s this uncomfortable, “ugh, why did I do that?” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Not like, I don’t know, some big planned thing, but if I’ve had a really bad night with food, the next morning I’ll feel like I have to sort of fix it.\n\nLike I’ll go run extra miles on the trail, even if I’m wiped out from work. There’s this trail near me that I usually like, actually—it’s kind of the one place I clear my head—but after those nights it doesn’t feel relaxing. It feels more like, “you have to do this because of what you ate.”\n\nOr I’ll skip breakfast before my shift, which is dumb because then I’m tired and cranky at work, and dealing with customers when you’re hungry is… not great. But in my head it’s like I’m trying to undo the night before.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I guess. Since I moved into this apartment and things kind of settled into a routine, it’s been… usually at least once a week there’s a late night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day—or sometimes that night—I try to make up for it somehow. Like skipping breakfast, making myself do a long run even if I don’t really want to, or… sometimes throwing up. I don’t like saying that. It’s not every single day or anything, but most weeks it happens at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Sometimes. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too lean?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, or at least I feel that way. Like, people at the venue have told me I look too lean, and I’ll kind of nod like, “yeah, okay,” but I don’t really take it in.\n\nIf my pants feel even a little tighter before a shift, my brain just goes straight to, like, everyone’s going to notice — coworkers, guests, whoever — and they’re going to think I let myself go. Which is ridiculous, because most guests are just asking where their seats are or complaining about drink prices, but I’m standing there thinking about my waistband.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it feels like proof, even if other people are telling me the opposite. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too lean?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, or at least I feel that way. Like, people at the venue have told me I look too lean, and I’ll kind of nod like, “yeah, okay,” but I don’t really take it in.\n\nIf my pants feel even a little tighter before a shift, my brain just goes straight to, like, everyone’s going to notice — coworkers, guests, whoever — and they’re going to think I let myself go. Which is ridiculous, because most guests are just asking where their seats are or complaining about drink prices, but I’m standing there thinking about my waistband.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it feels like proof, even if other people are telling me the opposite. So… yeah, I don’t think I see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating excessively because you're afraid of gaining weight or because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more the losing control part. I mean, the weight thing is always kind of buzzing in the background, but on those nights when I start eating, it’s like I’m not fully in charge anymore.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, okay, just put the container away, go brush your teeth, go do literally anything else… and then I’m standing there opening another thing anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from outside my body, which sounds dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nAnd then suddenly everything’s gone, and I’m just sitting there thinking, why didn’t I stop? It’s not even like I’m enjoying it by the end.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think… at least once. It got more noticeable after my apartment move kind of settled down, once I had a routine again. Which is funny, because I thought the routine would help, but late at night is when it happens.\n\nI’ll mean to just have something small, and then it turns into way more than I planned. Like, past the point where I’m even hungry. And then I panic and try to make up for it — throwing up, or skipping breakfast the next morning, or making myself go on this long run even if I’m tired.\n\nSo… yeah. Usually once a week, sometimes more, but most weeks at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating excessively?\n\nPatient: Not every time, but… sometimes. Like if I get home and I’ve eaten way too much takeout or snacks, I’ll end up in the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s usually because I get this panicky feeling that it’s just going to sit in me and turn into weight. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know, irrational, but in the moment it feels like I have to get it out.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating excessively?\n\nPatient: Yeah… like I said, not every time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much—usually takeout or snacks, stuff that’s just there—I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not a normal reaction, but in my head it’s like, if I don’t get it out, it’s just going to sit there and turn into weight. And then I get stuck on that thought.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of circling around it. The short answer is: sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after an episode of eating excessively?\n\nPatient: Right… yeah, sorry, I know you asked that already. It’s not after every time I overeat, but sometimes, especially if I get home and I’ve had too much takeout or snacks. I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s like I get stuck on the idea that if it stays in me, it’s going to turn into weight. I know that probably doesn’t sound logical, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too lean?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, or at least I feel that way. Like, people at the venue have told me I look too lean, and I’ll kind of nod like, “yeah, okay,” but I don’t really take it in.\n\nIf my pants feel even a little tighter before a shift, my brain just goes straight to, like, everyone’s going to notice — coworkers, guests, whoever — and they’re going to think I let myself go. Which is ridiculous, because most guests are just asking where their seats are or complaining about drink prices, but I’m standing there thinking about my waistband.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it feels like proof, even if other people are telling me the opposite. So… yeah, I don’t think I see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating excessively because you're afraid of gaining weight or because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more the losing control part. I mean, the weight thing is always kind of buzzing in the background, but on those nights when I start eating, it’s like I’m not fully in charge anymore.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, okay, just put the container away, go brush your teeth, go do literally anything else… and then I’m standing there opening another thing anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from outside my body, which sounds dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nAnd then suddenly everything’s gone, and I’m just sitting there thinking, why didn’t I stop? It’s not even like I’m enjoying it by the end.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think… at least once. It got more noticeable after my apartment move kind of settled down, once I had a routine again. Which is funny, because I thought the routine would help, but late at night is when it happens.\n\nI’ll mean to just have something small, and then it turns into way more than I planned. Like, past the point where I’m even hungry. And then I get stuck on that thought.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of circling around it. The short answer is: sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after an episode of eating excessively?\n\nPatient: Right… yeah, sorry, I know you asked that already. It’s not after every time I overeat, but sometimes, especially if I get home and I’ve had too much takeout or snacks. I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s like I get stuck on the idea that if it stays in me, it’s going to turn into weight. I’ll get this thought stuck in my head that it’s just sitting in me and it’s going to turn into weight, and then I end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up.\n\nI know you’re asking a pretty simple yes-or-no thing—so, no, not every episode. I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up because I get scared it’s just going to turn into weight.\n\nI know I’ve probably said that a few times—I just get kind of stuck on explaining it.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs making yourself throw up something you do more often than not after eating excessively?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / entertainment_attendant_or_related_worker / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compensatory_Behavior_Purging\nmanifestation: \"Sometimes after I get home and eat too much takeout or snacks, I go to the bathroom and make myself throw up so it won’t sit in me and turn into weight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say… not more often than not, no. It’s sometimes. Like if I get home and I’ve had too much takeout or I keep eating snacks, then my head starts going, you know, like it’s just sitting there and it’s going to become weight.

And then I’ll end up in the bathroom making myself throw up. But it’s not every time, or most times exactly… it’s more when that panic feeling kicks in.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess the main thing is my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. After late shifts, I’ll stop and get food—like a full takeout meal—and then when I get home I just keep going. Chips, sweets, whatever’s around in the apartment.\n\nIt’s weird because I work these odd hours, and by the time I’m done everything feels like it’s either closing or it’s all fast food, you know? I used to grab stuff after shifts with coworkers sometimes, but now it’s more like I’m by myself and I’ll eat way more than I meant to. Then afterward I kind of look at the wrappers or containers and I’m shocked, like… how did I eat all of that?\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after you've eaten all that food and seen the wrappers or containers?\n\nPatient: I feel kind of stunned, honestly. Like I’ll see the takeout container and the chip bag and maybe candy wrappers, and it’s just… I don’t know, it doesn’t feel like I meant to eat all that.\n\nAfter late shifts I’ll tell myself I’m just grabbing dinner, but then I get home and keep going with whatever’s around. And then I’m looking at it all afterward thinking, wow, that was a lot. It’s embarrassing, I guess. Also I hate wasting money on takeout—my apartment’s near this place that stays open late, and I keep saying I should just go straight home, but then… yeah. I end up shocked by how much I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word. I feel embarrassed, definitely, and kind of mad at myself, I guess. Like I’ll think, why did I spend money on all that when I’ve got groceries sitting there? I even bought those meal prep containers once—this whole plan to be organized—and they’re still in the cabinet.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe some guilt, but mostly it’s this uncomfortable, “ugh, why did I do that?” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Not like, I don’t know, some big planned thing, but if I’ve had a really bad night with food, the next morning I’ll feel like I have to sort of fix it.\n\nLike I’ll go run extra miles on the trail, even if I’m wiped out from work. There’s this trail near me that I usually like, actually—it’s kind of the one place I clear my head—but after those nights it doesn’t feel relaxing. It feels more like, “you have to do this because of what you ate.”\n\nOr I’ll skip breakfast before my shift, which is dumb because then I’m tired and cranky at work, and dealing with customers when you’re hungry is… not great. But in my head it’s like I’m trying to undo the night before.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to make up for eating a lot by doing something to counteract it?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I guess. Since I moved into this apartment and things kind of settled into a routine, it’s been… usually at least once a week there’s a late night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day—or sometimes that night—I try to make up for it somehow. Like skipping breakfast, making myself do a long run even if I don’t really want to, or… sometimes throwing up. I don’t like saying that. It’s not every single day or anything, but most weeks it happens at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Sometimes. Not every time I eat too much, but… yeah, sometimes after I get home and it’s like I’ve had too much takeout or snacks, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not… a normal thing to do, but in the moment I’m thinking I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. And then I feel gross about the whole thing.\n\nI guess it happens on those bad nights, mostly. Not every day.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… if I had to put a number on it, maybe like four times a month? Sometimes a little less, sometimes more, depending on how many of those nights happen.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I get this panicky feeling like I don’t want it just sitting in me, like it’s going to turn into weight. So I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate saying it that plainly, but… yeah, probably around once a week.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. Even when people at the venue say I look too lean or whatever, I still get really nervous if my pants feel a little tighter.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready for a shift and if the waistband feels different, suddenly I’m thinking everybody’s going to notice. Guests, coworkers… like they’ll look at me and think I let myself go. It’s dumb because most people are probably just trying to find their seats or buy a drink, but in my head it feels really obvious.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it can kind of ruin my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained weight even if it didn't affect your health?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would be. I mean, even if a doctor told me, “Your health is fine,” I’d still feel really upset if I gained weight.\n\nIt’s like at the venue, people will even say I look too lean sometimes, and I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel tighter before a shift, I start getting panicky, like everyone’s going to notice right away — guests, coworkers, whoever — and think I let myself go. Or if the scale is up even a pound, it sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then I’m distracted the whole night, even though I’m supposed to be helping people find seats or dealing with the usual chaos. So yeah… it would bother me, even if it wasn’t a health thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… significant? I don’t know. I don’t think there’s been some huge change, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I check sometimes, but then I kind of get in my head about normal ups and downs, like if I ate late after a shift or had a bunch of salty food. The schedule at the venue is weird too, so it’s hard to tell what’s real and what’s just, you know, one bad day on the scale.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I can point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you're too lean?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, or at least I feel that way. Like, people at the venue have told me I look too lean, and I’ll kind of nod like, “yeah, okay,” but I don’t really take it in.\n\nIf my pants feel even a little tighter before a shift, my brain just goes straight to, like, everyone’s going to notice — coworkers, guests, whoever — and they’re going to think I let myself go. Which is ridiculous, because most guests are just asking where their seats are or complaining about drink prices, but I’m standing there thinking about my waistband.\n\nAnd if the scale is up even a pound, it feels like proof, even if other people are telling me the opposite. So… yeah, I don’t think I see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating excessively because you're afraid of gaining weight or because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more the losing control part. I mean, the weight thing is always kind of buzzing in the background, but on those nights when I start eating, it’s like I’m not fully in charge anymore.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, okay, just put the container away, go brush your teeth, go do literally anything else… and then I’m standing there opening another thing anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from outside my body, which sounds dramatic, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nAnd then suddenly everything’s gone, and I’m just sitting there thinking, why didn’t I stop? It’s not even like I’m enjoying it by the end.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think… at least once. It got more noticeable after my apartment move kind of settled down, once I had a routine again. Which is funny, because I thought the routine would help, but late at night is when it happens.\n\nI’ll mean to just have something small, and then it turns into way more than I planned. Like, past the point where I’m even hungry. And then I panic and try to make up for it — throwing up, or skipping breakfast the next morning, or making myself go on this long run even if I’m tired.\n\nSo… yeah. Usually once a week, sometimes more, but most weeks at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating excessively?\n\nPatient: Not every time, but… sometimes. Like if I get home and I’ve eaten way too much takeout or snacks, I’ll end up in the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s usually because I get this panicky feeling that it’s just going to sit in me and turn into weight. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know, irrational, but in the moment it feels like I have to get it out.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating excessively?\n\nPatient: Yeah… like I said, not every time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much—usually takeout or snacks, stuff that’s just there—I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows it’s not a normal reaction, but in my head it’s like, if I don’t get it out, it’s just going to sit there and turn into weight. And then I get stuck on that thought.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of circling around it. The short answer is: sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after an episode of eating excessively?\n\nPatient: Right… yeah, sorry, I know you asked that already. It’s not after every time I overeat, but sometimes, especially if I get home and I’ve had too much takeout or snacks. I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s like I get stuck on the idea that if it stays in me, it’s going to turn into weight. I know that probably doesn’t sound logical, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after every episode of eating excessively?\n\nPatient: No, not every time. It’s more like… sometimes, usually after I get home and I’ve had too much takeout or snacks. I’ll get this thought stuck in my head that it’s just sitting in me and it’s going to turn into weight, and then I end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up.\n\nI know you’re asking a pretty simple yes-or-no thing—so, no, not every episode. Just sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up every time you have an episode of eating excessively?\n\nPatient: No, not every time. It’s more… sometimes, like after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, and then I start thinking about it sitting in my stomach. I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up because I get scared it’s just going to turn into weight.\n\nI know I’ve probably said that a few times—I just get kind of stuck on explaining it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up something you do more often than not after eating excessively?\n\nPatient: I’d say… not more often than not, no. It’s sometimes. Like if I get home and I’ve had too much takeout or I keep eating snacks, then my head starts going, you know, like it’s just sitting there and it’s going to become weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll end up in the bathroom making myself throw up. But it’s not every time, or most times exactly… it’s more when that panic feeling kicks in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think it can be at least once a week sometimes. Like, if I come home after work and I’ve grabbed takeout, and then I keep picking at snacks too, I start thinking about it just sitting in me.
